Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in the Court's entire opinion and judgment. He also concludes that South Carolina v. Katzenbach, which sustains the Act's coverage and preclearance machinery, and City of Rome, which sustains its operation against covered governmental units, foreclose the facial constitutional objection to the County's operative designation. This is distinct from the Court's statutory bar on collateral retrial of the coverage findings. He further states that the wholly judicial exception does not apply when a court adopts material electoral policy choices proposed, required or previously selected by the County or the State. Necessity and independent remedial authority must support any genuinely court-formulated temporary plan; he selects no plan and decides no purpose or effect question. No other Justice joins these additional formulations.

### 1996-11-18 — Ohio v. Robinette; No. 95-891

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** He would vacate the Ohio judgment and remand. He joins the no-script rule, the objective continued-seizure inquiry, and the Court's requirements to examine voluntariness and any necessary causal relationship to unlawful detention. A State cannot convert uninterrupted official restraint into consent merely by describing it as a request. Immediate questioning, documents, tone, positioning and the absence of an objective release signal bear on whether the motorist remained seized and whether the choice was real. A clear warning is strong evidence and may be required by state law or policy. He reaches no final admissibility result and preserves suppression where the applicable inquiries fail. He agrees to federal review and to setting aside the motive-based exit-order ground without adding a reason on those components; his chosen formal disposition remains vacatur, not reversal.

**Justice Ginsburg, concurring in the judgment.** She accepts federal review under governing Long despite her earlier preference in Evans for a different jurisdictional presumption. The Ohio opinion's interwoven analysis supplies no clearly independent state basis. She agrees that federal law requires no universal release script and that actual consent must be assessed under the total circumstances. Her agreement does not decide that Robinette's search was valid or that no detention continued. She emphasizes that Ohio may use its own Constitution to require a clear warning, provided it rests the rule on an independent state ground. Federal correction of this judgment does not displace that authority. She does not join Stevens's conclusion that this record already requires suppression.

**Justice Stevens, dissenting.** He accepts review and rejects an indispensable federal warning formula, but would affirm suppression on the actual encounter. His Bostick dissent's attention to the practical force of police authority, together with Royer and Brown's separation of consent from unlawful detention, leads him to treat the continued official direction after completion of traffic business as restraint requiring an independent justification. The deputy's question before departure continued that restraint; no separate suspicion or intervening circumstance supplies a lawful extension or breaks the connection to consent. Returning the license and Robinette's initial sense of freedom do not decide the effect of the subsequent direction. The deputy's courtesy, lack of a drawn weapon and Robinette's education bear on voluntariness, but furnish no intervening break from the detention. Stevens does not invalidate the initial stop or a lawful exit order because of hidden motive, and he proposes no automatic suppression rule after every illegality. His disagreement is with the Court's refusal to affirm suppression on this record, not with Ohio's freedom to require warnings under independent state law.

### 1996-11-25 — Hopwood v. Texas; Fifth Circuit No. 94-50664, 78 F.3d 932; no invented Supreme Court docket; OT1996, chunk 1, matter 6.

**Kennedy, concurring in part and concurring in the judgment.** Kennedy joins Parts I, III and IV–V but does not join Part II. He would assume a potentially compelling educational interest and invalidate the actual process for inadequate tailoring. Croson supports rigorous application without an unnecessary categorical prohibition; his Metro reasoning rejects assigning a contribution to an applicant merely from racial identity. The combination of different thresholds and separate decision rules remains unjustified despite individual exceptions within each track. He agrees that the appellate diversity prohibition must be removed from this remand but reserves the affirmative compelling-interest question. He neither requires identical numerical evaluation in every future program nor approves the later design.

**Scalia, concurring in part and dissenting in part; Thomas joins Section A.** Section A rejects nonremedial racial preferences as a justification for burdening an individual applicant. The equal-protection right belongs to the person, and educational aims do not turn racial ancestry into an entitlement or establish that group membership predicts educational contribution. The actual five-Justice Bakke Part V-C proposition is acknowledged; these Justices would expressly disapprove it to the extent it authorizes nonremedial preferences. They therefore dissent from vacating the diversity prohibition and from Part II, while joining the narrow invalidity holding and the individual causation/remedy provisions. They accept the same-decision allocation and preponderance requirement, but their substantive view excludes nonremedial preferences from the lawful counterfactual. Individual experiences may still be considered under lawful nonracial criteria.

In Section B, which Thomas does not join, Scalia declines the broader institutional rule and further remedial inquiry in Part III. Even assuming a broader responsible unit, the record does not establish that these preferences were necessary to remove a traceable barrier. He recognizes Fordice as existing authority and does not seek to overrule it, but would resolve this offered defense without the additional remand inquiry. Thomas instead joins Part III’s bounded recognition of the State’s duty; that join does not equate enrollment targets with removal of inherited barriers. Neither Justice supports a new injunction or punitive award, treats benevolent purpose as eliminating statutory intent, or deems every constitutionally valid remedial act forbidden merely because it considers race.

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and concurring in the judgment.** The Chief Justice joins Parts I–III, IV-D and V. He agrees with the individual-relief remand and punitive denial, without joining Parts IV-A–C. He would assess claimed individualization by actual operation: the order of decisions, thresholds, separate review, numerical aims, discretion, beneficiary categories, whether assumptions can genuinely be rebutted, and workable alternatives. A small formal weight for race is no answer when it determines outcomes near the admission threshold. Conversely, a program lacking quotas, separate tracks and numerical aims is not invalid merely because race is one consideration among several; no particular such program is approved. The combined 1992 design made race decisive across broad categories without the supported necessity and individualized fit he requires.

He understands a permissible educational objective to require concrete benefits from meaningful institutional inclusion, an explanation of the mechanism connecting the classification to those benefits, and attention to actual burdens. Racial arithmetic, generalized societal discrimination, reputation, hostility outside the institution, and assumptions that identity predicts viewpoint cannot substitute for that showing. For corrective measures he additionally requires objective evidence of the persisting effect, scope fitted to it, meaningful continuing opportunity for others, respect for legally protected existing interests, and termination when the basis ends. The State bears its justification burden. These additional formulations are his separate grounds, not additions to the Court’s controlling rule.

His causation position distinguishes the established unequal process from a claimed lost seat or monetary loss. A defendant may defeat admission-related compensation by proving that a specified lawful process would have produced the same denial, without erasing the violation. He would preserve nominal and declaratory recognition and fee-free lawful consideration if practically available, leaving the proper cause, defendant, immunity and precise relief for determination below. He agrees with the disposition of the separate statutory-intent component without adopting the Court’s statutory rationale.

### 1996-12-02 — Tyler Murray v. Montrose County School District RE-1J; Tenth Circuit Nos. 93-1466 and 93-1474; no Supreme Court docket assigned.

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela dissents. He would affirm the Tenth Circuit and reach the preserved geographic legal question while treating the individualized former-placement and later-progress disputes as moot. In his view, IDEA's preference for education with nondisabled children is distinct from geographic proximity: the regulations make proximity and the neighborhood school mandatory considerations, but permit another arrangement when the individualized IEP requires it. He would reject an independent burden-shifting neighborhood-school presumption without approving convenience-based transfers, diminishing the duty to use supplementary aids, or declaring distance irrelevant. A district's assertion that its specialized program is better would not establish compliance.

He would recognize section 1415(e)(2)'s additional-evidence channel in a live dispute, including relevant subsequent progress with its temporal relevance explained, while giving due weight to the administrative proceedings without surrendering independent legal judgment. He would order no new evidence here: the aids proffer does not establish the geographic-presumption theory, the independent unexhausted aids issue is not reached, and the later IEP removes the placement/progress remedy. He declines the wider evidentiary disagreement and any ruling on Northside's factual adequacy. His proposed legal answer and affirmance do not command a majority; the Court holds the controversy moot.

### 1996-12-09 — In re Askin; Fourth Circuit No. 94-5428, 47 F.3d 100; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied; October Term 1996.

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and in the judgment; no joins.** He agrees that the former wire/electronic exclusions cover the intercepted radio segment carrying both voices and that the 1994 deletion cannot retroactively make completed surveillance unlawful. His statutory position treats the place of acquisition as controlling under the former cordless exclusion. He would affirm the lower court's complete former-Title-III noncoverage ruling for that acquired segment and does not join the Court's additional oral-coverage route. Statutory noncoverage, on his view, neither authorizes interception at another point nor determines constitutional privacy.

He joins rejection of the categorical Fourth Amendment ruling and would state a further conditional rule: absent participant consent, a willing informant or knowing exposure, a landline or cellular caller does not surrender privacy merely because the other participant uses a vulnerable cordless handset without the caller's knowledge. *Katz* protects the speaker's own private words. *White* assigns the risk that an interlocutor will reveal or transmit them; it does not eliminate privacy merely because unknown agents can independently intercept a retransmission the caller did not select or know about. This rule leaves knowing exposure, participant consent and the cordless user's own expectation distinct. The record does not establish Askin's knowledge, so he would not finally apply the rule.

He accepts *Gelbard*'s statutory just-cause principle, but proposes a separate constitutional ground for this criminal-trial setting. When compelled questions are direct fruits of unlawful acquisition of the witness's own protected calls, and no recognized exception provides a lawful basis, refusal should constitute just cause under §1826(a). Coercing this speaker's testimony through his own unlawfully intercepted words gives the exclusionary rule a direct deterrent function. Question-specific derivation, personal rights and ordinary remedial exceptions keep the proposal bounded. *Pinson* and *Wellons* do not themselves establish this extension; *Calandra*'s grand-jury rule is left intact.

He would require the lower court to determine knowledge or knowing exposure, direct derivation of each question, and any preserved independent source, attenuation, objectively reasonable good-faith reliance or other lawful basis. If the governing premises establish lack of knowledge and direct derivation from unlawful acquisition without an applicable exception, the contempt adjudication must be set aside. He grants no automatic immunity from questions and no final discharge on this record. The Court's eight-Justice opinion leaves that independent constitutional just-cause question unresolved.

### 1996-12-10 — O'Gilvie v. United States; Nos. 95-966 and 95-977

O'Connor, joined by Scalia and Thomas, would exclude the punitive receipts. The physical injury is a legal cause of the damages, even though the award's purpose is punishment. The former text says on account of personal injury, while neighboring provisions use compensation language; the dissent rejects adding a compensation-only restriction. It distinguishes Schleier's employment backpay and injury-independent willfulness payment from a traditional tort award legally dependent on the physical injury. The dissent applies both statutory conditions and does not claim every tort-type receipt is excluded. Because the children prevail on that merits ground, the dissent does not decide their limitation defense; it also regards a choice between receipt and later clearance as unnecessary once mailing is rejected. All three agree that the father's appeal is timely.

Scalia separately retains the construction expressed in his Burke concurrence: the exclusion concerns physical or mental health injuries. This wrongful-death recovery satisfies that premise, unlike the employment entitlement in Burke. He joins the shared textual and causal dissent without converting his health-injury construction into the view of O'Connor or Thomas. Both dissents are noncontrolling.

### 1996-12-16 — Greene v. Georgia; No. 96-5369

Stone-Zsela agrees that federal collateral review and state direct appellate responsibility must remain distinct. Importing the former's deference can obscure the state court's duty to correct error without legal authority. He would vacate and remand so Georgia identifies the deference, if any, supplied by state law and then decides the federal exclusion claims. His remedy does not order a new sentencing proceeding; he expresses no view on any juror's qualification, harmlessness or the ultimate capital judgment. His disagreement concerns formal relief, not the Court's source-of-review rule.

### 1996-12-16 — M.L.B. v. S.L.J.; No. 95-853

Kennedy concurs in the judgment on due process alone. Boddie, Lassiter and Santosky identify the established parental interest and the need for fair procedures without creating a civil appeal in every case. Under Mathews, the permanent loss, risk of error, practical value of a usable record and governmental burdens must be assessed in the appellate structure Mississippi actually provided. Here the unaffordable record barrier defeats that review. He supports sufficient record access and reversal, but does not join the Court's extension of Griffin's equality rationale or treat wealth as a suspect classification. This is judgment-only agreement, not an additional opinion join.

Thomas, joined by Scalia, dissents on two grounds. Due process protects the established parental interest through an adequate hearing, but Boddie's indispensable initial access does not itself compel financing an additional civil appeal. Separately, Griffin and Mayer's criminal-access exception should not be extended beyond Kras and Ortwein's ordinary civil cost-allocation rule. Permanent termination is exceptionally grave, yet seriousness and the convergence of fairness and equality do not identify an additional appellate subsidy. The dissent questions the limiting principle for other civil claims while accepting the protected parental relationship. It neither finds the termination correct nor equates poverty with unfitness, and it leaves voluntary state support available. Griffin, Mayer and Bounds are not proposed for overruling; the dissent confines its claim to nonextension here.

### 1996-12-16 — United States v. Sahhar; Ninth Circuit Nos. 94-50186 and 94-50356; separate Supreme Court docket unassigned

Stone-Zsela joins the no-sentence-cap and state-placement holdings and the affirmance. He insists that continued federal custody requires clear and convincing proof of current mental illness causing current statutory danger; historical danger, incompetence alone or the dismissed accusation cannot substitute. Counsel, a neutral hearing, practical review and genuine unconditional or conditional release must remain available. He also expressly preserves release whenever the statutory release route requires it. He does not join Part V's distinct formulation of subsection (e)'s preponderance discharge determination or its detailed procedural account, and his concurrence supplies no different universal rule for every discharge motion.

He separately concludes that the limited custody-defined class and the interests in safely winding up lawful custody, treatment and public protection are sufficiently related even under intermediate scrutiny, without choosing that tier as generally governing. He does not join the Court's separate equality formulation. Transfer efforts must be reasonable and periodically renewed; actual state acceptance ends federal retention. The remand must test current predicates and Arizona's actual response, leaving dismissal intact. His concurrence decides no freestanding enumerated-power question and authorizes neither nominal review nor permanent custody based on old findings.

### 1997-01-06 — DeAngelis v. El Paso Municipal Police Officers' Association; Fifth Circuit No. 93-8700, 51 F.3d 591; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and in the judgment; no joins.** He joins the retaliation holding but rejects statutory insufficiency on hostile environment. The repeated sex-derogatory message, distribution to more than seven hundred colleagues, connections to DeAngelis, recurring comments and workplace incidents, humiliation, claimed loss of authority, promotion deterrence and refusal to identify the columnist permit a rational jury's severe-or-pervasive finding under Harris. Physical threats, supervisory power, reduced performance and the written medium are not statutory barriers. He therefore reaches the preserved expressive defense.

In his view, this award punishes protected editorial opinion or satire solely for its sex-derogatory viewpoint. Such civil liability is content based and is not narrowly tailored here to the compelling interest in eradicating sex discrimination. The publication itself is the independently asserted wrong; the record supplies no separate discriminatory employment act, true threat, authority-backed directive or denial of employment or associational rights to which the expression is integral or evidentiary. Hurley preserves the expressive inquiry, and Mitchell distinguishes punishment of protected belief from use of expression to establish an independently unlawful act. He would prohibit Title VII damages resting solely on such protected opinion or satire, while preserving liability for discriminatory decisions, quid pro quo demands, true threats, authority-backed directives and expression integral to or used as evidence of independently unlawful employment conduct. His rule is confined to this expressive application and does not declare that speech cannot itself be workplace harassment. That constitutional ground alone supports his hostile-environment affirmance. On retaliation, he finds no material employment or membership disadvantage caused by the complaint and leaves stronger threats, harassment and public-shaming cases open.

**Justice Stevens, concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by Justice Ginsburg.** They join Part II but would reverse the hostile-environment portion and remand. Harris protects disadvantageous conditions without proof of lost output. The lower opinion's reliance on destroyed opportunity, palpable performance deterioration, missing advances and absence of a superior harasser materially narrowed its review of the jury verdict. Its correct abstract recitation of Harris and the substantial contrary contextual evidence do not cure that error. Repeated sex-based attacks, broad circulation, mocking, humiliation and alleged impairment of command warrant review without those added thresholds. Ginsburg's Harris explanation makes professional disadvantage relevant without measurable productivity loss. They find a review error, not liability as a matter of law. Mitchell and Hurley keep the preserved expressive defense distinct; it and any remaining properly preserved grounds must be resolved before liability could stand. They would restore neither damages nor a particular allocation and reject both automatic newsletter immunity and automatic loss of protection for an offensive editorial.

### 1997-01-06 — United States v. Watts; United States v. Putra; No. 95-1906, one Rule 12.4 petition concerning two Ninth Circuit judgments

**Justice Scalia, concurring; no joins.** He joins the bounded Court opinion, which does not say that every punishment below a statutory ceiling is necessarily punishment only for the conviction. His additional disagreement concerns Commission authority. In his view, §994(b)(1)'s requirement of consistency with pertinent provisions of Title 18 and §3661's protection of sentencing-information consideration forbid a Commission or judicial rule excluding otherwise lawful acquitted-conduct consideration. The Commission may recommend congressional change; it may not itself prohibit a lawful enhancement or impose an otherwise unsupported higher burden solely because the conduct was acquitted. That statutory objection is his own; Thomas's agreement with the narrow constitutional opinion supplies no join to it.

**Justice Breyer, concurring; no joins.** He joins the Court's construction of the Guidelines as written and its constitutional holding. He regards the use of acquitted conduct in mandatory enhancement as a legitimate subject of Commission policy change. Different burdens make judicial findings compatible with acquittals, but that is not the only permissible sentencing-policy consideration. The institutional significance of a jury's acquittal may support excluding that conduct from mandatory enhancement even though the present relevant-conduct provisions contain no such exception. He disagrees with Scalia's proposed statutory restriction on the Commission. Neither concurrence changes today's calculation rules.

**Justice Stevens, dissenting; no joins.** He seeks plenary briefing and argument and would, if the merits are decided now, affirm both sentence-vacatur judgments. Section 3661 permits receiving information and using it in lawful sentencing discretion; it does not itself assign mandatory weight or authorize a binding-range increase on a preponderance finding of additional criminal conduct. Section 1B1.4 addresses selection within the range or departure, while §1B1.3 determines the range through relevant conduct. Section 994(l)'s instruction about incremental punishment for multiple convictions reinforces his narrower authorization reading; the Court's contrary view treats that instruction as a minimum rather than an exclusive limit.

The criminal proof tradition and his McMillan dissent make the mandatory punitive consequence central. His Witte dissent likewise rejects the notion that a broad statutory maximum erases the concrete increment. He distinguishes discretionary use of information from facts that enlarge a binding range, and would not sustain these increases on the existing preponderance findings. In Watts, possession and use differ; his objection does not depend on a finding that the jury rejected possession. He announces no categorical ban on considering an acquitted episode, no independent Grady successive-prosecution bar, and no comprehensive new jury-allocation test. Lawful resentencing may use information in selecting an authorized sentence, but may not simply reimpose these mandatory increases on the same insufficient authorization-and-proof premise. He sets no sentence and would disturb no acquittal.

**Justice Kennedy, dissenting; no joins.** He would receive full merits briefing and oral argument before resolving this important recurring question. Dowling and Witte make the Government's objection to a categorical bar persuasive, and circuit conflict supports review. But Witte expressly reserved acquitted conduct; mandatory sentencing gives the distinction practical force, and disagreement among the reversal supporters about the Commission's authority exposes an unsettled statutory issue. Summary correction with reservations does not provide the examination he considers necessary. He neither affirms the defendants' merits position nor adopts a reasonable-doubt rule; his present dissent concerns the procedure of deciding the merits now.

### 1997-01-07 — Old Chief v. United States; No. 95-6556

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and dissenting in part; no joins.** He joins the Rule 403 holding and reversal because the comparison is between two means of proving the same complete legal status, not between proof and no proof. Ordinary prosecutorial narrative control has limited force for status detached from the charged episode. An unconditional, legally complete admission supplies that element, while identifying the similar assault adds no narrative link to present possession and creates acute propensity danger. He preserves the Government's ability to prove a distinct nonpropensity purpose under Rule 403 and rejects any general right to stipulate away event narrative or require acceptance of partial or qualified admissions.

He differs on remedy. In his view, the close resemblance to violent conduct and the unnecessary character information prevent the Government from showing harmlessness on this record. He would vacate the conviction and remand for a new trial rather than leave harmlessness open. At retrial the Government could use the adequate admission or another sanitized means for status; prior-offense details could be introduced only for a distinct lawful purpose satisfying Rule 403. His prejudice conclusion and mandatory new-trial remedy command no additional join and do not change the Court's five-Justice remand.

**Justice O'Connor, dissenting, joined by Justices Scalia and Thomas.** They would affirm because the authentic judgment directly proves a statutory element. A defendant's offer neither removes that element nor makes ordinary specific proof irrelevant; the Government retains its burden and its legitimate interest in persuasive evidence. Huddleston's safeguards and the Rule's substantial-outweighing requirement preserve meaningful trial discretion. They recognize the similar-assault propensity danger but would not find the name and basic nature of this qualifying conviction inadmissible as a matter of law merely because an admission proves the same legal status. They question the compulsory-substitution consequence and the sharp distinction between status proof and narrative proof, without treating relevance as conclusive admissibility or prosecutorial choice as immune from Rule 403.

They do not rely on the curable instruction wording as a new ground, and they do not treat the credibility instruction as waiver of the separate objection. Once the Court's error rule governs, they accept the ordinary Williamson/Kotteakos harmlessness process and oppose automatic retrial; that conditional remedy position is not a vote to find error or reverse. No omitted-element constitutional error or Roy substitute standard is claimed.

### 1997-01-13 — Walmer v. Department of Defense; Tenth Circuit No. 93-3377, 52 F.3d 851; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied

**Justice Scalia, concurring, joined by Justice Thomas.** They join both Court parts and regard the specific military relationship as sufficient. They also draw on Bowers's recognition of authority to regulate the specified conduct: a reasonable conduct-based personnel policy may rest on a permissible moral judgment as well as military discipline. Their Romer dissent distinguished such judgment from a bare wish to harm persons. They do not claim that Bowers already decided equal protection, that every private aversion is legitimate or that the Court has overruled Romer. Beach's tolerance for reasonable predictions and imperfect fit supports this narrower service policy. No individual-performance finding or least restrictive alternative is required. This additional conduct-morality ground is theirs alone and is not attributed to the other three equality affirmers.

**Justice Souter, concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by Justices Stevens and Ginsburg.** They join the attainder holding but would vacate the equality-based denial and remand. Heller and Romer require examination of the connection between this actual disadvantage and a legitimate objective. Rich concerned fraudulent denial on entry under another regulation, so treating it as conclusive leaves this admitted-acts classification insufficiently examined. Stevens's Beach position likewise requires a relationship to the actual distinction, and Ginsburg's Virginia position supports applying the proper inquiry without importing that case's sex-classification tier.

They accept substantial deference to reasonable military predictions, the availability of conceivable legitimate reasons and Walmer's burden to establish entitlement to preliminary relief. They find legal inadequacy in foreclosure by the older case, not proof that the Army lacks a rational basis. They demand neither actual-purpose proof from the Government, heightened scrutiny, an individualized impairment finding nor a new evidentiary hearing. On remand the courts should reassess the merits question under ordinary rationality and address irreparable injury, comparative harm and public interest if the renewed merits assessment makes them material. Those factors were never found satisfied. They would neither enjoin separation automatically nor finally invalidate the policy.

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and dissenting in part; no joins.** He joins the legislative-predicate attainder holding and would vacate the equality-based denial under a different inquiry. His Part A treats mandatory separation for homosexual acts as an intentional sexual-orientation classification, whether described as conduct or a deliberate status proxy. Bowers does not exempt it from equality review. Romer supplies an ordinary-review floor and a warning against unexplained class disadvantage, but does not itself allocate a justification burden to the Government. He would make that doctrinal change here: the Government must identify an actual military objective and establish a real relationship between it and the intentional classification, without relying on moral disapproval, private bias or stereotype.

His reason is that merely conceivable explanations would permit this intentional disadvantage without establishing its actual connection to the military function invoked. Professional judgments and reasonable contemporaneous predictions about discipline, cohesion, readiness and command receive substantial but nonconclusive weight; an assertion of deference does not carry the Government's burden. Experience and institutional knowledge may suffice without scientific certainty or a universal formal-findings requirement. Part A independently supports his remand because the lower courts treated the claim as foreclosed, placed the practical burden on Walmer to disprove the rule, and did not resolve the military justification his proposed allocation requires.

His separable Part B additionally adopts intermediate scrutiny: the Government must identify an actual important military objective and establish a substantial relationship to it, with substantial weight to supported professional judgment. Neither Romer nor the public-university sex cases already supplies that tier. Separating Part B leaves Part A's independent Government burden intact.

He retains Bowers's specified-conduct holding, ordinary regulation of materially relevant conduct and the absence of a constitutional entitlement to service. He requires neither least restrictive means nor an individual exception in every case, and he does not infer that military concerns lack support from the absence of an individual performance finding. The district court should assess the military showing on a developed record and apply all preliminary-relief requirements. He would order no injunction, immediate status change or final invalidation. Any concrete personnel commitments or settled arrangements must be shown in the actual record and given their proper remedial weight; abstract command responsibility, convenience, institutional preference or expected continued exclusion does not establish protected reliance. He makes no finding here that such commitments exist and adds no automatic transition requirement. His Part A burden and Part B tier each remain noncontrolling.

### 1997-01-14 — Abebe-Jira v. Negewo, No. 95-8873; Eleventh Circuit No. 93-9133.

Justice Scalia, joined by Justice Thomas, concurs in Parts I and V and dissents from the damages judgment. They accept the statutory forum and reject a categorical political-question bar, but find no sufficient congressional authority for the asserted domestic damages action. The prohibition of torture does not itself authorize a federal cause of action. A forum for independently actionable law-of-nations torts does not necessarily authorize judicial recognition of every later norm; the common-law setting and lower-court practice are the strongest affirmative arguments, but do not establish the needed source for these Justices.

Suter requires an actual source of private liability; O'Melveny rejects the manufacture of a federal rule from general federal interests; Meyer keeps jurisdiction and substantive action distinct. Franklin presupposed a cognizable action, and their concurrence there relied on a narrower enacted-remedies ground. Central Bank's binding received-securities holding remains confined to its established setting, and Musick, Peeler concerns incidents of already recognized liability. Neither supplies the missing ATS premise. Sanchez-Espinoza's domestic-immunity result is not a categorical bar to foreign-official suits. Scalia and Thomas would reverse the damages judgment and remand on the absence-of-action ground, rejecting both compensation and punishment through that ground. They announce no categorical objection to punitive damages in an otherwise authorized intentional-tort action, do not approve torture, and do not reach an unpleaded TVPA claim, limitations or appointed-counsel question.

### 1997-01-21 - Babbitt v. Youpee, No. 95-1595; OT1996.

Authority: [Record](../records/Babbitt_v_Youpee_merits_1997-01-21.md).

Justice Stevens dissents. His concurrence in Irving accepted Congress's power to impose consolidation conditions but objected to their abrupt application without a reasonable opportunity to arrange affairs. Texaco v. Short supports allowing an owner time to avoid a legislated loss. Youpee died nearly six years after the October 30, 1984 amendment, which preserved lifetime use and transfer, permitted devise to an existing co-owner, and narrowed the affected interests through its earnings provisions. Stevens regards those changes and that opportunity as sufficient to distinguish Irving.

The difficulty of transmitting these interests to family members is substantial, but Stevens would not turn ordinary beneficiary choice into a right to continue existing fractionation indefinitely. The fact that these particular devises do not create additional fractions does not foreclose legislation directed at reducing the existing problem. He would reverse and remand on the presented takings ground, without ordering a new probate distribution or deciding an individualized notice claim. He does not find that Youpee actually knew of the statute, culpably abandoned the interests, or held property of no value. He also rejects extending Lucas to every restriction of a separate property attribute.

### 1997-01-21 - Elizabeth G. Curtis v. School Committee of Falmouth; historical petition identifier No. 95-617; no separate merits docket assigned; OT1996.

Authority: [Record](../records/Curtis_v_School_Committee_of_Falmouth_merits_1997-01-21.md).

No separate writing is filed.

### 1997-01-27 - ProCD, Inc. v. Zeidenberg; Seventh Circuit No. 96-1139, 86 F.3d 1447; District Court judgment, 908 F. Supp. 640. No separate Supreme Court docket number assigned.

Authority: [Record](../records/ProCD_v_Zeidenberg_merits_1997-01-27.md).

No separate writing is filed.

### 1997-02-18 - Bibles v. Oregon Natural Desert Association; No. 96-713; OT1996, chunk 3, matter 31.

Authority: [Record](../records/Bibles_v_Oregon_Natural_Desert_Association_summary_review_1997-02-18.md).

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in the Court's controlling explanation and the requirement of a new Exemption 6 balance, but dissents from vacatur to the limited extent that he would reverse the Ninth Circuit's judgment and remand. He applies FLRA and Reporters Committee unchanged: access to private recipients for competing advocacy is not itself information about the Bureau. His position leaves the weight of privacy and any genuine recipient-selection evidence for the lower court and does not direct final nondisclosure. His writing gives no distinct legal ground for choosing reversal rather than vacatur.

### 1997-02-18 - California Division of Labor Standards Enforcement v. Dillingham Construction, N.A., Inc.; No. 95-789; OT1996, chunk 3, matter 32.

Authority: [Record](../records/California_Division_of_Labor_Standards_Enforcement_v_Dillingham_merits_1997-02-18.md).

No separate writing is filed. The Court's opinion does not revive an unlimited reading under which every relationship to a covered plan establishes preemption.

### 1997-02-18 - General Motors Corp. v. Tracy; No. 95-1232.

Authority: [Record](../records/General_Motors_v_Tracy_merits_1997-02-18.md).

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and dissenting in part; no other Justice joins.** He joins Part I on General Motors's own assessment injury. He would reverse the judgment insofar as it sustains the tax on the competitive commodity-gas purchases and remand for nondiscriminatory treatment. His comparison focuses on the transaction bought by a noncaptive industrial customer: LDCs and interstate marketers can compete to supply that commodity even though LDCs also serve captive customers. West Lynn's examination of the actual fiscal preference and Oregon Waste's rule against unjustified discrimination require a connection between the favored transaction and the service burden said to justify it. In his view, a blanket seller exemption lacks that connection on this record. This is a disagreement over the legal significance of the established competitive commodity purchase, not a finding that particular unregulated special contracts existed. He would preserve local-distribution regulation and fitted support for documented backup, low-income, credit or distribution obligations through nondiscriminatory means, including a neutral subsidy, cost-based credit or exemption confined to regulated bundled service. State courts would select lawful consequent tax relief, including a refund only where actual payment and governing remedial law support it, or a lawful neutral adjustment. He would not decide equal protection.

**Justice Stevens, concurring in part and dissenting in part; no other Justice joins.** He joins Part I but would vacate and remand the Commerce Clause merits for an actual industrial-market comparison. West Lynn requires examination of how the linked fiscal scheme operates; Carbone prevents equal treatment of local and interstate outsiders from curing a preference for a favored local service. Regulated obligations are material, but in his view do not justify closing the inquiry into sales competing for noncaptive buyers. He would not find unregulated contracts, calculate cross-subsidies or conclusively invalidate the exemption on this sparse record. If the comparison establishes discrimination, Ohio must satisfy the existing justification rule; any compensatory defense must meet Fulton's actual-burden, approximate-equivalence and equivalent-events requirements. He would reserve equal protection and order neither a particular subsidy nor a predetermined refund.

### 1997-02-18 - Ingalls Shipbuilding, Inc. v. Director, Office of Workers' Compensation Programs; No. 95-1081.

Authority: [Record](../records/Ingalls_Shipbuilding_v_Director_OWCP_merits_1997-02-18.md).

**Justice Scalia, concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by Justice Thomas.** Both join Part I's refusal to forfeit the death-benefit claim based on predeath releases. They would reverse only the ruling that the Director is a party respondent. In their reading, Rule 15 names the agency whose order is reviewed; here that entity is the independent Benefits Review Board. The Department's common administrative umbrella does not turn the Director into the Board or make its order his. A command to name the agency whose order is reviewed does not independently confer substantive party capacity on every officer designated to represent the Department. Section 921a and the regulation address representation where capacity exists, and cannot create the missing capacity; section 921a also expressly excepts proceedings in this Court. Rule 1(b)'s prohibition on enlarging or diminishing jurisdiction corroborates the limit on using procedural rules to create an entitlement to participate. Newport News's restriction on independent review and reservation of respondent status do not supply an affirmative respondent right. Continuing adversity between the employer and widow establishes the controversy but does not answer who may enter it as a party. The Director may present the Department's position as an amicus, a nonparty friend of the court; genuine special-fund or financial-interest cases present different questions. Their objection does not undermine the private appeal, require forfeiture of the widow's claim or decide those other settings.

### 1997-02-18 - Federal Election Commission v. Legi-Tech, Inc.; D.C. Circuit Nos. 94-5379 and 95-5085, 75 F.3d 704; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.

Authority: [Record](../records/FEC_v_Legi_Tech_merits_1997-02-18.md).

No separate opinion is filed.

### 1997-02-18 - Uhlrig v. Harder; Tenth Circuit No. 94-3190, 64 F.3d 567; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.

Authority: [Record](../records/Uhlrig_v_Harder_merits_1997-02-18.md).

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in the judgment. He would reach the constitutional merits under Collins's workplace-duty boundary and a proposed rule for the reserved affirmative-danger claim. He would require an official's affirmative creation or material increase of a specific foreseeable danger, or knowing obstruction of available protection, with conscious disregard of a known serious risk when time and practical opportunity permit a response, and a causal connection to the injury. Assurances, reliance and imminence may bear on that inquiry but are not universally necessary elements; he would add no undefined independent conscience-shocking requirement after those conditions are proved.

On this record, closing the unit and delegating placement were affirmative policy acts, but the warnings, file access and clinical process do not demonstrate the personal culpable contribution his rule requires. No named defendant is shown personally to have selected Waddell's destination, given Uhlrig a concrete assurance inducing abandonment of available protection, concealed a known immediate threat or personally overridden clinical safeguards to direct her into the encounter. He does not find that all officials lacked awareness or that the decision was sound policy. The gravity of the harm and possible negligence cannot alone make senior administrators constitutional insurers.

He would preserve liability questions involving concealment of an imminent threat, an affirmative direction into a uniquely dangerous encounter after a safety assurance, or a personal override of safeguards with deliberate indifference. Independently pleaded federal rights and genuinely different compulsory or intentionally harmful conduct remain outside his no-general-duty reasoning. Because his merits inquiry finds no constitutional violation, he does not decide qualified immunity. His proposed framework and application are noncontrolling.

### 1997-02-18 - United States v. Brockamp and United States v. Scott; No. 95-1225, one petition embracing Ninth Circuit judgments reported at 67 F.3d 260 and 70 F.3d 120.

Authority: [Record](../records/United_States_v_Brockamp_merits_1997-02-18.md).

No separate opinion is filed.

### 1997-02-19 - Auer v. Robbins; No. 95-897.

Authority: [Record](../records/Auer_v_Robbins_merits_1997-02-19.md).

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in part and in the judgment. He joins validity, practical exposure and correction, but would replace the controlling-weight component of Thomas Jefferson University and the corresponding general Seminole Rock rule. He acknowledges that he joined TJU and that it already protects plain text, consistency, explanation and the actual agency ground. His disagreement concerns the remaining proposition that the administering agency's selection among permissible meanings controls because it wrote and administers the regulation.

He would have courts determine meaning independently using ordinary interpretive tools. If genuine uncertainty remains, an authoritative and considered agency interpretation should receive special persuasive weight when it is consistent or adequately explained, fairly noticed, within delegated authority, grounded in relevant expertise and more than a restatement of the statute. Weight would follow those reasons, rather than agency authorship alone. An interpretation may neither enlarge statute or regulation nor impose liability without the notice required by law. Agency amicus participation neither makes its reading controlling nor automatically disqualifies it as litigation invention.

In his view, delegation to prescribe an exemption differs from the judicial responsibility to determine the adopted rule's meaning. Expertise may persuade, and an actual delegation may assign policy discretion, without every ambiguity transferring interpretive authority. He accepts the Secretary's practical-exposure reading independently because it fits the text, salary concept and the distinction between an abstract sanction, an effective policy and an actual practice. He likewise accepts the correction provision's alternatives and mandatory reimbursement and future promise. He does not reach the separately asserted affirmative-burden contention because he regards it as inadequately preserved.

His proposed change would preserve lawful delegated policy choices, operative regulatory text, ordinary reasoned-decisionmaking requirements, review of the agency's actual ground and the earlier decisions' independent substantive holdings. He would not reopen TJU's completed reimbursement judgment or declare its actual construction substantively wrong. Statute-specific allocations of authority require separate examination. Concrete reliance involving settled transactions, compensation arrangements and fairly communicated obligations deserves weight; an administrative preference for controlling deference is not itself that reliance. This record identifies no concrete reliance requiring a different result where independent interpretation reaches the same salary construction and correction mechanism. He makes no finding about reliance across other programs, authorizes no general reopening, and leaves distinct retrospective-liability claims to their applicable notice, finality and remedial rules. This proposed doctrinal change is noncontrolling.

### 1997-02-19 - Lynce v. Mathis, No. 95-7452; October Term 1996, chunk 4, inventory No. 41.

Authority: [Record](../records/Lynce_v_Mathis_threshold_1997-02-19.md).

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in the judgment; no other Justice joins his writing. He agrees that exhaustion comes first and that the Court should not decide the Ex Post Facto merits on this record. His following view is conditional on the courts lawfully reaching those merits and decides nothing for the Court.

Applying Weaver v. Graham, which protects a legally available means of reducing confinement, and California Department of Corrections v. Morales, which protects meaningful mandatory reconsideration without requiring proof that the lost hearing certainly would have produced parole, he would hold that Florida violated the Ex Post Facto Clause when retrospective cancellation of release credits already awarded and used to release Lynce lengthened his confinement. The objective operation of the credit system determines the punishment effect; its original overcrowding purpose does not negate the actual return to prison. Florida used the credits to calculate and end imprisonment, then applied an offense-based cancellation backward and reincarcerated Lynce. That direct increase is more than the lost opportunity protected in Morales despite the Board's initial individualized prediction. A credit determining lawful release forms part of the effective measure of confinement; describing it as administrative does not erase its effect.

This position does not freeze every prison-administration rule, protect credits never awarded or lawfully unavailable at the offense date, or bar prospective changes that do not increase punishment for completed conduct. If the merits are reached, he would have the district court determine the protected amount under the correct temporal analysis, order release if the lawful sentence has expired, and recalculate any remaining confinement without the unconstitutional retrospective cancellation. He does not demand certainty that an omitted hearing would have yielded release. Those conditional merits and remedial views impose no present credit award or release command.

### 1997-02-19 - Maryland v. Wilson; No. 95-1268; OT1996, chunk 4.

Authority: [Record](../records/Maryland_v_Wilson_merits_1997-02-19.md).

**Justice Kennedy, dissenting, joined by Justice Stevens.** Stevens and Kennedy would affirm because the passenger's added restraint needs an objective circumstance connected to the actual stop. Mimms's driver committed the traffic offense and controlled the vehicle; a passenger did neither. Aggregate danger does not justify an otherwise arbitrary command to every innocent passenger. Officers retain practical latitude to act on supported safety circumstances, but an explanation tied to those circumstances must precede the exercise of additional control rather than depend on the officer's grace after categorical authority is conferred. The movements and nervousness are not denied; the State did not preserve them as an independent justification, so they supply no basis to concur in reversal.

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, dissenting.** He would permit an exit direction when any objective circumstance reasonably supports it for safety, facilitating a lawful search or conducting the stop. The showing is modest and may arise from movements, numbers, location, conduct or other practical facts; neither probable cause nor a formal suspicion label is required. Mimms's driver-offense predicate does not by itself justify an added command to a passenger. Whren's ordinary stop authority should therefore remain subject to a reason connected to the actual stop, rather than general statistics or administrative ease. These observations might have supplied such a reason if properly preserved, but the Court cannot replace the State's categorical case. He would leave suppression in force and order no remand for a new justification. He reserves commands to remain in a vehicle and preserves lawful-search control and arrest on probable cause.

### 1997-02-19 - Regents of the University of California v. Doe; No. 95-1694; OT1996, chunk 4.

Authority: [Record](../records/Regents_v_Doe_merits_1997-02-19.md).

No separate opinion is filed.

### 1997-02-19 - Schenck v. Pro-Choice Network of Western New York; No. 95-1065; OT1996, chunk 4.

Authority: [Record](../records/Schenck_v_Pro_Choice_Network_merits_1997-02-19.md).

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring.** He joins the Court's fixed/mobile distinction and removal of the automatic retreat. In his view the supported repeated close pursuit permits a narrower provision on remand: preserve an initial peaceful approach, but after an objectively clear verbal or nonverbal refusal require the enjoined defendants to stop following and maintain a modest, precisely defined separation supported by the record. Direct conduct rules can address obstruction, touching, threats and harassment without making every speaker calculate an automatic moving fifteen-foot boundary. He would permit, not require, that carefully fitted provision. It would restrict neither nonparties, willing conversation, the initial peaceful approach, stationary advocacy nor speech addressed generally, and would create no undefined personal-space right or general listener's veto.

**Justice Scalia, concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by Justices Kennedy and Thomas.** Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas join the Court's independent conclusion that the moving restraints fail Madsen and that their dependent distance must fall. They would invalidate the fixed expressive restrictions as well. Their stricter approach requires a legally supported remedial predicate and direct justification for restraining protected expression. The existing conduct prohibitions and substantial TRO compliance make the added expressive exclusion doubtful; appellate assumptions about what access relief might justify cannot replace the trial court's actual assessment. In particular, treating cessation as a discretionary counseling privilege does not establish its actual necessity when the lower rationale relies on unwilling listeners' personal space. General safety interests cannot by themselves establish entitlement to a private judicial remedy. They preserve lawful obstruction and physical-interference restraints and do not claim a right to threaten or coerce. Their disagreement with fixed relief is consistent with joining the sufficient Madsen ground against the moving restraints.

**Justice Breyer, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** He joins the qualified fixed access and counseling rulings but would leave the lower judgment intact pending the District Court's interpretation and possible modification of the disputed mobile terms. The lower court expressly left their geographic reach and particular applications unresolved and retained an ordinary route for clarification, including problems arising from escorts or overlapping encounters. In his view contextual construction and first-instance administration should precede invalidating the disputed operation. He does not approve an unlimited radius, supply a travel cap himself or conclude that every literal floating zone is constitutional. His disagreement concerns the Court's decision to invalidate now on this posture.

### 1997-02-24 - Brown v. Hot, Sexy & Safer Productions, Inc.; First Circuit No. 95-1275, 68 F.3d 525; no separate Supreme Court merits docket assigned; OT1996, chunk 4.

Authority: [Record](../records/Brown_v_Hot_Sexy_Safer_Productions_merits_1997-02-24.md).

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring.** He joins the three granted holdings and would also reject the procedural-due-process and constitutional Free Exercise theories. Assuming the written positive-subscription policy created a state-law interest, the plaintiffs failed to preserve a developed argument that Massachusetts postdeprivation remedies were inadequate. A general assertion that the exposure cannot be undone does not preserve that distinct challenge. He therefore does not decide whether the planned departure from the policy was random and unauthorized under Parratt and Hudson, or instead implicated Zinermon's concern with foreseeable deprivation through delegated authority. Nor does he find that a remedy compensates every possible harm. The local policy's written parental-permission requirement and the statute's separate religious opt-out from instruction on disease retain their own terms; possible state-law relief is not adjudicated.

On constitutional religion, he distinguishes compulsory listening from compelled profession or religiously forbidden conduct. These plaintiffs were not required to perform, affirm, disclose private information or submit to bodily intrusion, and no religious exercise was prohibited, penalized or conditioned on abandoning belief. Applying Smith's neutral, generally applicable rule in that setting, he finds no constitutional Free Exercise violation. Yoder's protected religious family direction and Lukumi's prohibition on religious targeting remain distinct; neither offense at an idea nor curricular labeling alone decides whether religion has been burdened. His position does not authorize establishment, targeting, pretextual curriculum or coercive participation, and does not treat public-school attendance as a waiver of parental instruction.

He reaches no RFRA temporal or substantive rule and supplies no new ground concerning the independently dismissed request for prospective declaratory relief. Because he identifies no constitutional violation on his analysis, he does not decide qualified immunity. His additional procedure and constitutional religion grounds have no other Justice's join and do not enlarge the Court's three holdings.

### 1997-02-25 - Dunn v. Commodity Futures Trading Commission; No. 95-1181.

Authority: [Record](../records/Dunn_v_CFTC_merits_1997-02-25.md).

No separate opinion is filed.

### 1997-02-26 - United States v. Wells; No. 95-1228; respondents Jerry E. Wells and Kenneth R. Steele.

Authority: [Record](../records/United_States_v_Wells_merits_1997-02-26.md).

**Justice Stevens, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** He agrees that the Court may decide the statutory issue and that acquittals and independent trial matters retain their proper limits, but would construe §1014 to require materiality and affirm the new-trial judgment. The received meaning of false-statement offenses, the statute's objective and the consolidation assurance support retaining that limitation; Congress's omission of the word does not alone show an intention to abolish it.

In his view, a speaker's purpose to curry favor differs from a statement's objective capacity to matter to the institution. The majority's culpability requirements therefore do not answer the risk of imposing serious criminal liability for trivial flattery or an inconsequential lie. His Aguilar and X-Citement positions support examining the offense's received legal meaning and object, rather than grammar alone. Williams and restraint in construing serious criminal prohibitions reinforce the narrower reading. Kay rejected an actual-reliance defense under its predecessor; objective materiality does not require proof that the decisionmaker actually relied on the statement.

Once materiality is retained, Gaudin requires the jury to decide it. Respondents contested that issue and the judge expressly removed it from their jury's deliberations. Stevens would preserve the new-trial judgment on that record, not order acquittal or declare every omitted-element error automatically reversible. Roy permits harmlessness review within its category but does not establish harmlessness of this different withdrawal. His position leaves the two acquittals intact and decides no independent instruction, indictment, evidentiary, conspiracy or sentencing issue unnecessarily.

## Public procedure after completed actions

### 1996-10-21 — Department of State v. Legal Assistance for Vietnamese Asylum Seekers, Inc.; No. 95-1521

The Supreme Court review concludes with a limited remand to the D.C. Circuit. The next act is consideration of §633 and any surviving grounds and relief under the governing legal standards. No asylum proceeding is opened and no foreign protection is extended.

### 1996-11-04 — California v. Roy, No. 95-2025; October Term 1996, chunk 1.

Supreme Court review concludes with vacatur and remand to the Ninth Circuit. The next act is review of the Mannix claims under the Court's qualified Chapman method, including relevant instructions, evidence, findings and genuine preservation limits; District Court proceedings may follow if otherwise proper. The Court does not require a particular result or a new evidentiary proceeding. Conditional relief, if warranted, must permit a reasonable retrial opportunity. The Clark conviction and reversed special-circumstance punishment remain outside the disposition.

### 1996-11-06 — Lopez v. Monterey County; No. 95-1201

The Supreme Court appeal is completed. The district court must enforce nonuse, determine the actual submission history, require expeditious use of the designated preclearance channels and consider any independently authorized interim arrangement. It must assess any traceable continuing remnant and the fit of a proposed response insofar as needed for that counterfactual or properly presented relief.
